### **@Leo_oe_I** | 👍 24 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgyIIpFekxEHm4AKLGl4AaABAg)

I like Professor Xueqin and this is an overview course, so it's not surprising to get the peace-loving interpretation of the Indus Valley Civilization (IVC). I've heard it from Western professors myself as well, and in his defence, he left room for the interpretation being wrong, so this isn't intended as a heavy criticism or take-down of him in particular because it's not one of those situations in which he diverges significantly from a common, if declining, consensus. However, the peaceful model is based on some misconceptions drawn from early work and speculation on the Harappan culture that many non-specialists still believe to be true. There are, in fact, quite a lot of weapons at IVtC sites that were interpreted as tools because of their placement in domestic settings, their simplicity of design and their thinness (e,g,, their lack of the reinforcing central rib that many bronze weapons require to prevent bending). The problem with that is that their are seemingly _only_ domestic buildings in such sites and the fact that they're not in caches may simply indicate a feudal-like system in which the heads of households are called-up for military duty and are required to possess a weapon. They're described as "tools," but it's not like they're just hammers and saws: they're axeheads, knives, and spearpoints. Their simplicity and thinness is also not unique. Egyptian weapons were quite simplistic during this time as well and sufficiently dangerous. I won't be exhaustive in order to keep this short, but one more common misconception is the lack of signs o desctructive violence like burning. The reason there are no signs of fires at the height of their power is actually testament to the destruction waged in their lands. During the transition from the early to mature IVC, many settlements were abandoned and new ones founded. The mature cities we often discuss like Harappa and Mohenjo-daro show no signs of destruction because they were only founded relatively recently in the so-called mature period (and were surrounded by walls and bastioned btw). Why did they abandon their old settlements for these new urban areas? Because they were burnt to the ground. It may or may not be from warfare, but a lack of destruction by fire surely can't be used as evidence of peace. The best we can say is that they appear to have been a _relatively_ peaceful and egalitarian society.

### **@nil0355** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=Ugw-cwkD3XE_K8hn49B4AaABAg)

What happened in ancient India is some kind of a synthesis of the the indegenious pre Steppe migration culture and Steppe culture. Steppe brought masculine, warrior gods , fire worship, hierarchical social division but the religions which grew up Vedic, Buddhist, Jain were very broad minded and gave individual freedom to choose one's spiritual discipline. Also, the different philosophies and religions debated, argued without need for violence. And this is one the most fascinating aspect of Indian culture and what makes the history of Hinduism, Buddhism, Jainism so different from Abrahamic religions. In time the Steppe religion had to change due to internal pressure. The heavily ritualised priest centric worship which was focused on gaining heavenly hedonism changed to focus on devotion and love and personal relationship with the Divine. At the same time the Brahmanical hierarchical system also preserved its legitimacy. So what's transpired, and it is still an ongoing process, is that of a dialectics between a more universal, liberal, values and more rigid, conservative approach. So it is actually very difficult to understand the culture. Just as one could say Bramhinism dominated the indegenious liberal egalitarian culture, similarly one could argue exactly the opposite, that the indegenious culture forced Brahminism to modify and take within its fold universal principles.

### **@priyasingh9847** | 👍 41 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=Ugy5NwmxHahRRcH0S8J4AaABAg)

Professor I am completely hooked on your lectures. I teach a course on the Indus Valley Civilization (IVC) at the undergraduate level. Religion at the IVC level largely comprised of worshiping the 'Mother Goddess' or 'the Fertility Goddess" or 'Elements of Nature' , a practice which was meant to ensure protection against the elements and vagaries of Nature and to secure a bountiful harvest( It is the surplus harvest in the rural IVC sites near the Indus and its tributaries, that ensured the diversification of economic occupations, and enabled the urban section of the population to take up trade and craftsmanship as their profession in the City Centers. The bountiful harvests and the consequent Trade  led the IVC to rise above the mere subsistence levels to establish itself as the first Urban , Bronze Age civilization of the Indian Subcontinent, a phenomenon termed as 1st  Urbanization).  While the discovery of the Great Bath at Mohenjodaro in the higher Citadel area is largely seen to illustrate the practice of ritual bathing( performing ritual ablutions before the morning prayer remains central to Hinduism to this day)  , a figure identified as  proto-Shiva on an Indus Valley seal only points to the practice of animism which came naturally to the Indus Valley social set up. In no way can the Indus Valley religion be described as  proto-Hinduism or proto-Buddhism. The Sangha was the defining feature that institutionalized and played a key role in the dissemination of Buddhism, and this feature was established by Buddha himself , which was consequently revitalized by the Buddhist Councils under the subsequent monarchs, most prominent being Asoka who championed it as State Religion post BCE 262-261 Kalinga War. What we can state more convincingly is that later day Hinduism retained many features of the Indus Valley religious practices. But Buddhism and its ruminations on Dhamma or moral righteouness were a feature of the 2nd Urbanization milieu that was beset by material afflictions and tyranny of the Brahmanical class.

### **@sg6qd** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgzcyoiFrscaYv4oFLl4AaABAg)

Good lecture but there are a few flaws in the reasoning. Explanation of the religion part has plenty of misinformation. For eg., Siddharth Gautama as the first Budhha is entirely wrong, Yoga being born after Buddha and then being adopted by Hinduism also wrong (there is archaeological evidence of Yoga in Indus valley - look at the Pashupatinath Seal that is a man sitting in a yogic pose and another archaeological site is Balathal in Western India where you can find a yogic pose samadhi skeleton in 700 BCE that is 100 years earlier to Buddha). Also, there is no hierarchy among Hindu gods compared to the folk gods. Yes they assimilated but we're branded as avatars of the main 3 Brahmanical Gods - Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesh (Shiva). Also - Plato travelled to Taxilla (ancient India) and learnt about Buddhism and Hinduism of the time.

Hinduism has never been a priest-controlled system like a church. While Brahmins preserved Vedic rituals, dharma and moksha were always open to everyone through paths like bhakti, yoga, and jnana. Local gods weren’t erased by a “trinity” but embraced and integrated which is why they are still worshipped today. ### **@namanydv_garud** | 👍 94 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgwOtGV8YZLIi4e6F4p4AaABAg)

It was an interesting video. I loved it! 
I am from India, and if you see the initial teachings in the Vedas and the core of Hinduism, there wasn't discrimination at all. You can choose your occupation based on your nature, and different classes were created to solidify the teachings and create specializations, an easy work process, and flow. 


However, it was not until the last few thousand years that some corrupt individuals attempted to divide people and consolidate power in their own hands. Buddhism was one revolution against that, but other improvements and processes that returned to the roots also took place. 

By the way, one fun fact that the professor forgot to mention is. There was an Iranian Vedic period akin to the Indian Vedic period. And you can argue that both countries were once closely connected. 
However, after a battle of ten kings mentioned in the Rigveda (the oldest religious text), a few clans were defeated, and one of them was the Parshu clan. And from that clan, Persia was created. (Parsu means axe since they primarily fought using axes.) 


Now, the real game changer was the Vedic king of gods, Indra, who was believed to have helped Sudas (the king who won the battle Dasrajyam), brought a flood and drown many Parsu clan warriors and the allied clan warriors, and then later the Parsu people were thrown back in Persia; and they created Zoroastrianism. 


And you can see in Zoroastrianism that they consider devas as evil (in Hinduism, devas are the gods, and Indra was the king of devas) and ashuras (the evil celestial beings in Hinduism) as the good ones. 


Other practices, such as worshipping fire, the soma (nectar) ceremony, and many other aspects, are kinda the same. 

Hope you guys learn something new. Feel free to drop questions or correct me if you have solid proof or evidence. 
Take care!

### **@Curiosity1220**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgziiQubGXzuah22zXp4AaABAg)

Professor Jiang has suggested that the religion of the Indus Valley Civilization may have contained elements that later appeared in Buddhist traditions, raising the possibility that aspects of the Indus religious culture could have been Proto-Buddhist. In Buddhist tradition, preserved in texts such as the Pali Canon, there is a lineage of 28 Buddhas, with Siddhartha Gautama regarded as the 28th Buddha.

It is also widely acknowledged that Proto-Indo-Iranian migrations likely occurred during or after the late phases of the Indus civilization. However, no inscriptions in Vedic Sanskrit have been discovered anywhere in the Indian subcontinent prior to the 3rd century BCE. The inscriptions of Ashoka are generally regarded as the earliest deciphered inscriptions in the Indian subcontinent. These inscriptions are written in Prakrit using the Brahmi script, sometimes described in Buddhist contexts as Dhammalipi.

Certain Indus artifacts have also attracted scholarly comparison with later ascetic traditions of South Asia. For example, the so-called Pashupati seal depicts a figure seated in a posture that has been interpreted by some scholars as a yogic or meditative posture, and it also includes a wheel-like symbol that some have compared with the Dhamma Chakra, a symbol later associated with the sramana traditions of the Indian subcontinent. Similarly, the statue often referred to as the “Priest-King” has been noted for its robe-like garment, which some observers have compared to later monastic attire, although the precise interpretation of this figure remains debated.

The earliest surviving manuscripts of the Vedas date from roughly the 11th to the 14th centuries CE. These manuscripts are written in Classical or Vedic Sanskrit using the Sharada script, and include commentarial traditions associated with the Hindu Brahmin scholar Sayana. The Sharada script itself is generally dated to around the 8th century CE.

Mainstream scholarship proposes that the Vedas were transmitted orally from approximately the 2nd millennium BCE until the medieval period, a span approaching two millennia. While this oral-transmission model is widely accepted among Hindu Vedic scholars, the absence of earlier manuscript or inscriptional evidence continues to generate discussion regarding the historical processes through which these texts were preserved and transmitted.

> @ronshinoda899 Which Hindu manuscripts are claimed to have been ‘burnt’, and what primary evidence supports this claim? The earliest surviving manuscripts from the Indian subcontinent are predominantly Buddhist. These include the Gandharan Buddhist Manuscripts (c. 1st century BCE–1st century CE) written in Gandhari Prakrit and Kharoshti script, the Spitzer Manuscript (c. 2nd–3rd century CE) composed in Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit, the Bower Manuscript (c. 4th–6th century CE), and the Bakhshali Manuscript, a mathematical text dated between the 3rd and 7th centuries CE. These manuscripts reflect linguistic traditions such as Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit and regional Prakrits like Gandhari.  
>   
> In contrast, the earliest surviving manuscripts of the Vedas—composed in Vedic or Classical Sanskrit and written in scripts such as Sharada—generally date much later, approximately between the 11th and 14th centuries CE. Therefore, claims that earlier ‘Hindu manuscripts’ were destroyed require clear archaeological or manuscript evidence, since the extant manuscript record currently indicates that the oldest surviving written materials from the region are primarily Buddhist.  

> **@ronshinoda899**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgziiQubGXzuah22zXp4AaABAg.ATwf99UaMlsAU0V5nrmO1E)  
>
> this doesn't imply Hindu scriptures like the Vedas are "later" overall—it's a selective focus on material preservation that ignores the profound differences in transmission methods, composition timelines, and historical destruction.   
>   
> Hindu texts like the Rigveda were composed orally centuries (or millennia) before being committed to writing, thanks to a rigorous mnemonic system (e.g., pada-patha recitation) that ensured near-perfect fidelity over generations. Buddhism, emerging later, adopted writing earlier for proselytization across diverse regions.  
>   
>   
> Rigveda was composed ~1500–1100 BCE, based on linguistic analysis (archaic Indo-Aryan vocabulary, no Iron Age references), astronomical allusions (e.g., Pleiades positions), and intra-textual geography (e.g., Sarasvati River as a flowing entity, pre-dating its drying ~1900 BCE). This predates even the late Vedic layers like Atharvaveda (~1000–800 BCE).  
>   
> Hinduism's emphasis on shruti (heard/revealed knowledge) delayed widespread scripting to avoid "pollution" of sacred sounds. When written, they used perishable palm-leaf or birch-bark in humid climates, prone to decay. Buddhist texts, conversely, were inscribed on durable birch-bark in arid northwest India/Pakistan and prioritized for monastic copying.  
>   
> Tibetan monk Dharmasvamin's 1235 CE account corroborates the devastation of hindu texts at nalanda, noting surviving Hindu scholars fleeing with fragments. This obliterated countless Vedic commentaries, Upanishads, and astronomical texts. Now don't also call him a member of the rss.   
>   
> Inscriptions (e.g., Ashoka's 3rd BCE edicts reference Vedic rituals) and foreign accounts (Megasthenes' Indica, ~300 BCE, describes Brahmin Vedic reciters) confirm pre-Buddhist existence. Linguistic phylogeny places Vedic Sanskrit as the root of Prakrits used in early Buddhist texts.  
>   
> Radiocarbon dating of Nepalese Rigveda leaves (~1040 CE) matches paleographic analysis, but genetic studies of oral transmission (e.g., 2016 PNAS paper on Vedic chants) show <0.1% variation over 3,500 years—proving stability without early writing.  
>

> **@Curiosity1220**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgziiQubGXzuah22zXp4AaABAg.ATwf99UaMlsAU0cSHDs6m1)  
>
> @r@ronshinoda899 The inscriptions of Ashoka do not mention or endorse any Vedic rituals. Instead, they refer to both Brahmanas and Sramanas as respected religious groups or pasandas within society, emphasizing ethical conduct (dhamma) rather than ritual orthopraxy. Likewise, the Greek ambassador Megasthenes, in his work Indica (preserved through authors such as Strabo and Arrian), refers to the ‘Brachmanes’ as a class of philosophers, priests, and advisors within Indian society. However, his account does not explicitly describe Vedic recitation, Vedic chanting traditions, or specific Vedic sacrificial rituals. Instead, his observations focus primarily on their social role, intellectual reputation, and advisory relationship with rulers.  

> **@Curiosity1220**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgziiQubGXzuah22zXp4AaABAg.ATwf99UaMlsAU0gmqjheGU)  
>
> @ronshinoda899 Historical reconstruction is based primarily on verifiable evidence such as archaeology, inscriptions, manuscripts, and contemporaneous written accounts. Across the Indian subcontinent, a substantial body of early material evidence is associated with Buddhist traditions. Archaeological sites such as the stupas at Niglihawa Stupa, Gotihawa Stupa, Sanchi Stupa, and Piprahwa Stupa, along with rock-cut monastic complexes like the Ajanta Caves and Kanheri Caves, provide substantial archaeological testimony to the development of Buddhist institutions. Major monastic universities such as Nalanda Mahavihara, Vikramshila Mahavihara, and Odantapuri Mahavihara further illustrate the scale of Buddhist scholastic and institutional life, while excavations at Kumrahar have revealed structures interpreted as charitable or medical establishments associated with monastic communities.  
>   
> Epigraphical evidence also plays a crucial role: the inscriptions of Ashoka—written in Prakrit, Greek, and Aramaic using scripts such as Brahmi, Kharoshthi, and Greek—represent some of the earliest decipherable inscriptions from the subcontinent and document the ethical policy of Dhamma. In addition, accounts of Buddhist pilgrims such as Faxian, Xuanzang, Hyecho, and Yijing provide detailed descriptions of monasteries, education, and religious life in India.  
>   
> While oral traditions may preserve cultural memory, historians generally regard them as methodologically weaker because they can change across generations. Therefore, historical conclusions rely primarily on material, epigraphical, and textual evidence rather than on purely 2000 years oral claims.

### **@JonoftheChords** | 👍 24 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=Ugzcb8Mib1Kv7nPcGgN4AaABAg)

Prof Jiang is not proposing that Buddhism as founded by Siddartha Gautama per se was the religion of the IVC. He’s saying an older, different religion with similar tenets of enlightenment, karma, true reality, and compassion was the religion of the IVC. But we have no name for it, so he called it Proto-Buddhism.

With regard to Buddhism being a “response” to the threat of Brahmins to Kings, he’s not saying Siddartha Gautama Buddha came up with Buddhism to oppose Hinduism/the caste system/priesthood. It just so happens that Buddhist ideas contradict the power structure of the Brahmins. Leading King Ashoka, if seen through a cynical lens, opportunistically promoted/championed Buddhism.

Remember that Siddartha Gautama has said that thousands of Buddhas (enlightened beings with no desire, who are able to see reality perfectly, without delusion, independent of the “religion” of Buddhism) existed before, but were lost to history.

Hope this resolves the issue of Prof Jiang appearing to get the chronological order wrong.

Vedanta: “Who am I?”

Respectfully, I think the professor is wrong about some parts of IVC history and Hinduism.

Buddhism starts around 600 BCE. Caste consolidation happens in the post-Mauryan to Gupta era (roughly 200 BCE to 650 CE, with rigidification continuing up to around 1400 CE in many regions). So there is a gap of many centuries between the origins of Buddhism and full caste consolidation. Endogamous caste system as we know it was not yet in place.

Brahmans are priests, accountants, scientists, philosophers, and intellectuals. They form roughly 1–5% of the population (numbers vary by region). Traditionally they were not allowed to own much property, and the majority of them were poor. Since large-scale caste consolidation and rigidification happened only after roughly 650 CE, the caste system wasn’t fully systemic or oppressive until then. Until that point, every caste was primarily occupational, and switching occupations (and thus jātis) was still possible to a significant extent. What later became labeled the “lowest castes” originally included people in various skilled occupations like metallurgy, pottery, or cooking. I dont think that brahmans were some all controlling groups.

What I know about the end of the IVC is that the Saraswati River (Ghaggar-Hakra) dried up, and people migrated eastward to the Ganga-Yamuna plain. I don’t know anything about a slow genocide.

I think the Professor may have painted with a broad brush but you see he looked at the smorgasbord of Indic religions from a structural-functional perspective, royal patronage of Buddhism, and frankly it was refreshing. One thing I will mention:
- Veneration of the hearth (ie fire) is or was a phenomenon in North China especially among certain minority peoples.
- Zoroastrianism had an impact across China. Some large Buddhist temples would have an annex for fire-worshippers.
-the influx of Persians into China during the Tang, also Tang China controlled parts of Persia briefly, a vast Central Asian realm under Tang control, via the bottleneck of the Silk Road. 
- An illiterate Persian fluent in Chinese in the Tang Dynasty around the 7th Century, An Lushan led a rebellion centered at Yangzhou a major trade hub, helped by Sogdian Zoroastrians that almost overthrew the Tang and certainly weakened it spelling its doom. There followed a purge of Zoroastrians and they were Sinicized by forced marriages.
- How was An Lushan defeated? Fight fire with fire. The Tang Emperor had Peroz III, son of the last Sassanian King of Persia, as a Tang Sassanid General under his command. Tang China had a bunch of Sassanid refugees who fled there after the Arab invasion of Persia around 651 AD. The Tang Zoroastrian Sassanid mercenaries helped defeat the Zoroastrian Sogdian Persian forces of the Persian traitor An Lushan in and around Yangzhou. What an amazing history - a many hued tapestry, that China possesses.

### **@seculardharma**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=Ugz5_3lTcf08gjDv3n94AaABAg)

I like professors explanation but I think he is misinformed about some aspect of Hinduism and Buddhism evolution. The Hinduism that indo aryan followed is completely different different from the ones followed today. Even the main gods weren’t same. Vedic Hinduism believed in Indra, vayu, Agni and other nature based god. Shiva, krishna and Brahma which are the trinity in modern Hinduism is mostly not based on these other gods. The idea of brahmand, dharma and karma evolved from a recorded revolutionary movement called as shraman movement: which gave rise to non-Vedic Hinduism (I.e cults of shiva, Vishnu and shakti) ; Jainism and  Buddhism. What the shraman movement stood against was ritualism rather than caste system. It also gave rise to vegetarianism in India. 

Caste system didn’t become rigid much later in medieval India during the end of Gupta empire. It was informed by rise of feudalism in India. Sikhism and present Hinduism was formed much later due to the bhakti movement which was anti caste system.

### **@Kalki0025** | 👍 7 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cvI8rukoda8&lc=UgzoZVqrx0OMzNPxbI94AaABAg)

​Don’t you think the theory that the Indo-European language originated in Russia is somewhat half-baked? Recent studies suggest that the Indo-European language originated in western Iran. Genetic studies have discovered a Caucasus Hunter-Gatherers component that spread to the steppe and a similar Iranian Neolithic component (in the Zagros) that spread eastward. This component was found in Bronze Age Anatolia without steppe ancestry.



The Yamnaya were roughly 1/3 CHG or Iran_Neolithic. Iran_Neolithic ancestry comprises 40-60% of all modern Indians. Don’t you think Iranian Neolithic farmers migrated toward Russia around 7,000-8,000 years ago, forming the Yamnaya culture? Perhaps these people originated from Mehrgarh or Bhirrana in the Indian subcontinent, as India and Iran were better suited for human settlement 10,000 years ago than Russia, which experienced a cold and temperate climate heavily influenced by the last Ice Age.



The cow is significant in Indo-Aryan culture. The Indo-Europeans were central to the domestication of the bull/cow, the invention of the wheeled wagon, dairying practices, and milk consumption. However, the cow was domesticated in the Indus Valley Civilization over 9,000 years ago and in Anatolia separately. If one believes the steppe people domesticated the horse and then entered India, this migration never affected the genetic lineage of Indian cow breeds. Cattle genetics conclusively prove that no admixture of Taurine cattle into India has ever occurred. It seems silly to think that Indo-Aryans came to India without a single cow.



If we look at Russia, the Proto-Uralic language formed around the same time as the Indo-European language in relatively close proximity without any connection between them. In the Caucasus region or near the Black Sea, there are unrelated Kartvelian languages that formed around the same time or earlier than Indo-European.



Do you think the Burushaski language in northern Pakistan might be related to a pre-Proto-Indo-European language, given that haplogroup R2 is significantly present among the Burusho people? Haplogroup R2 is estimated to have emerged around 15,000 years ago, likely in the Indian subcontinent, while R1 originated in Central Asia and R itself in West and South Asia. It seems likely that the Indo-European language originated in Iran, the Indus Valley, or the BMAC culture.

Not to offend you, professor, but you are damn wrong in your opinion about hindu principles. Brahmins had never ever any power. Some privilege they got was exclusive right to conduct yagnas as priest. Yagna was not criteria to attain moksha ever,at best it was described as necessary karma for dwij (brahmins kshatriya vaishya, shudra). Moksha had to be attained through knowledge or selfless karma, as described in upnshadas sankhya brahmsutras bhagvad geeta. Yagna guaranteed heaven not moksh, as a matter of fact texts motivated people to see beyond heaven and attain moksha. Further, what is evidence that Buddhism had invented yoga? Hinduism predates Buddhism, and Hinduism had always yoga as it's one of core philosophy (other than sankhya and vedanta). Yoga is so central to Hinduism it is impossible to separate one from other.

The Kshatriyas (Kings) were the most devoted followers of Sages and priest so much so that the entire folk tales, epics and historical records showcased the Kshatriyas as the defender of Dharma.
Buddhism failed on its own as it leads to nowhere but Nihilism and complete apathy to the material needs and injustices. Check out Buddhas's view on women and why Devi worship was critical to the triumph of Hinduism over Buddhism.

So Buddhism was created by kings and warriors cast to abolish brahmins’ power or because of intrinsic longing for Oneness? These are two different motivations. Neither Buddha nor his disciples proclaimed king’s power sacred. Moreover, Buddha renounced his power as prince and became a monk.
